Not quite. A hearsay objection can be made when an examining attorney asks the witness to repeat an out-of-court statement made by someone else in an attempt to utilize said statement as proof of the truth of what it asserts. Since the attorney is asking Ms. Duber what she herself saw, an objection of hearsay would be overruled in this instance. [[Try Again->Leading Example]]Not quite. A narrative objection can be made when an examining attorney asks an overly broad question which invites a long-winded answer. The question that the attorney asked Ms. Duber, however, is quite specific and would not elicit a very long answer. [[Try Again->Leading Example]]Not quite. Speculation is an objection made when an examining attorney invites the witness to theorize or infer conclusions which they cannot directly testify to. In this case, we already know that Ms. Duber was at the scene of the crime, so the attorney is not asking her to speculate on anything she does not know. [[Try Again->Leading Example]]Correct! The issue with the previous testimony is that the woman speculated about the reason behind the attack by accusing her husband of ordering it without proof. By rephrasing her testimony and grounding it in her own observations and lived experiences, the woman can successfully evade the speculation objection while still comminicating the same information. [[Proceed->Completion]]Not quite. The previous testimony was objected to because the woman speculated about the reason behind the attack by insisting that her ex-husband had ordered it. In order to avoid the objection of speculation, the woman cannot testify to anything she did not directly observe or experience. [[Try Again->Rephrase the Testimony]]Not quite. This objection can be made when the information solicited by an attorney’s question is meant to draw attention to an involved party’s past behavior or actions in an attempt to suggest that the party acted consistently with their established character in the present case. The question asked by the attorney in this instance has nothing to do with the defendant's past behavior; it is focused on primarily on forensic evidence. [[Try Again->Opinion Example]]Not quite. A scope objection can be made when a question asked by an examining attorney solicits information not available in the case briefing (across case facts and witness affidavits). Given that previous testimony has already established several pieces of evidence that fall within the scope of the case, drawing a conclusion from said pieces of evidence would not fall beyond the scope of the trial. [[Try Again->Opinion Example]]Close --- but not quite. The speculation objection can be made when an examining attorney invites the witness to theorize or infer conclusions which they cannot directly testify to. Though, technically speaking, Mr. Samson would have to make an inference in order to answer this question, the nature of the question itself does not call for speculation. In other words, a witness with enough knowledge about munitions, bullet casings, and gunshot residue would be able to answer this question without theorizing or guessing beyond their expertise. [[Try Again->Opinion Example]]Not quite. As discussed previously, a leading question is one in which the examiner strongly hints at the answer they would like to receive from the witness, with said answer usually being a "yes" or "no." In this case, the question cannot be answered with a simple "yes" or "no," and does not seem to be soliciting an "intended" answer from the witness. [[Try Again->Admissible Question Example]]Not quite. The opinion objection can be used when an attorney asks a question which requires expertise or specialized knowledge to answer that the witness does not possess. However, asking a witness to describe behavior that they personally observed does not call for an opinion. In this instance, the attorney is free to ask Ms. McMillan to "describe" the friend's behavior because doing so does not require any analysis or conjecture. [[Try Again->Admissible Question Example]]Not quite. The relevance objection is utilized when it is unclear how a question asked by an examining attorney is relevant to the case at hand. The bar for a question to be deemed "irrelevant" by a judge is fairly high, however; the question must be either highly trivial or blatantly tangential to the case at hand. Any reasonable person would understand that learning about the friend's behavior following the confrontation may provide insight regarding her connection the incident, meaning a relevance objection would not be sustained. [[Try Again->Admissible Question Example]]Not quite. A hearsay objection may be made against a witness who recounts an out-of-court statement made by another person for the truth of what that statement asserts. This objection would be valid if Ms. McMillan relayed what the friend had said to her over the phone; however, since her testimony describes the friend's manner of speech rather than the contents of their conversation, the hearsay objection does not apply. [[Try Again->Speculation Testimony Example]]Not quite. A witness is considered nonresponsive if they refuse to answer a question or if the answer they provide seeks to dodge or distract from the question being asked. In this instance, Ms. McMillan directly answers the question asked by the attorney by describing the friend's manner of speech on the call (shaky breath, disoriented manner of speech). [[Try Again->Speculation Testimony Example]]Close --- but not quite. An opinion objection may be made against a witness who volunteers an evaluation which they are not qualified to provide. However, whether or not Ms. McMillan has expertise in a specific field has no bearing on the admissibility of this testimony. [[Try Again->Speculation Testimony Example]]Not quite. This question is objectionable! Try thinking about how this question plays into the claims the prosecution is trying to prove about Mr. Ketkar. [[Try Again->Argumentative Example]]Not quite. The badgering objection can be made when an examiner is growing overly combative or confrontational with a witness. Though the question presented to Mr. Ketkar is a touch accusatory, it is not worded in a way meant to irritate, belittle, or demean like most badgering questions are. [[Try Again->Argumentative Example]]Not quite. You're right in that the question asked is a leading question: it can be answered with a simple "yes" or "no," and the inclusion of the word "surely" strongly hints to the witness which answer the attorney is looking for. However, you have to keep in mind that leading questions are permitted during cross examination, and are thus not objectionable! [[Try Again->Argumentative Example]]Not quite. A witness is considered combative when they are overly aggressive, rude, or confrontational to a questioning attorney, typically during cross examination. However, though Mr. Ketkar is trying to avoid providing information which may be used against him, he is not being rude or hostile to the attorney himself. [[Try Again->Nonresponsive Example]]Not quite. An opinion objection may be made against a witness who volunteers an evaluation which they are not qualified to provide. However, none of Mr. Ketkar's testimony in his response would require him to be qualified in a specific field. [[Try Again->Nonresponsive Example]]Not quite. If the witness infers or theorizes on case details without having the appropriate expert background to do so, their testimony can be stricken from the record with a speculation objection. Though Mr. Ketkar is speaking hypothetically in this instance, he is not theorizing about specific case details; he is simply explaining why he finds the prosecution's argument unlikely. [[Try Again->Nonresponsive Example]]Not quite. The argumentative objection can be made when a question asked by an examiner serves not to extract information from the witness, but rather to advance their own argument. Because the argumentative objection can only be used in response to an examiner's question, it cannot be used in this scenario. [[Try Again->Combative Example]]Not quite. The badgering objection can be made when an examiner is growing overly combative or confrontational with a witness. Since the objectionable material in this instance is witness testimony and not an examiner's question, the badgering objection cannot be used. [[Try Again->Combative Example]]Not quite. Unlike the previous scenario, Mr. Ketkar does answer the question here, if succinctly: "Badly." Even though he volunteers other information not related to the question, the fact that he answered the question at all renders the nonresponsive objection obsolete. [[Try Again->Combative Example]]Correct! The previous question was considered argumentative because it contained the attorney's assumption that the defendant was visibly poor, rather than allowing that conclusion to be made naturally through witness testimony. This rephrased question eliminates the attorney's assessment while opening up the possibility for the defendant to make that connection themself. [[Proceed->Rephrase the Question 2]]Not quite. The previous question was deemed "argumentative" --- meaning it contains the attorney's own evaluation rather than acquiring it neutrally from the witness. This rephrased question still contains the attorney's assumption that the defendant was visibly poor, meaning it is still argumentative. [[Try Again->Rephrase the Question 1]]Not so fast! Even though the way in which this question was presented was objectionable, this doesn't mean that it can't be rephrased in an admissible way. The question is doing genuine persuasive work for the defense that they would be remiss to abandon. [[Try Again->Rephrase the Question 1]]Correct! The main issue with the previous question was that the attorney was asking the teacher to repeat an out-of-court statement made by an uninvolved party (the inspector) to prove that the neighbor was aware of the risk posed by the tree. However, without having the inspector as a witness in the trial who can be cross-examined, any statements he made cannot be put on record as evidence. The best thing for the attorney to do is move on to a different question. [[Proceed->Rephrase the Testimony]]Not quite. The previous question was objected to on grounds of hearsay because it cannot be answered without the witness directly relaying what the inspector --- who is not an involved party --- had said to the neighbor, which is an out-of-court statement that could prove the neighbor was aware of the risk posed by the tree. The only way to get this testimony through would be to call the inspector to the stand as a witness himself. [[Try Again->Rephrase the Question 2]]In a trial centered on a violent confrontation between a girlfriend and boyfriend, several witnesses who personally know the two are called to the stand by both sides to provide testimony. One of these witnesses, Rebecca McMillan, is a dear friend of the girlfriend who has long been vocal about her distaste for the boyfriend. Ms. McMillan recounts the phone call she received from another one of the girlfriend's friends immediately after the confrontation. The attorney then asks, "How would you describe [the friend]'s behavior on the call?" What objection could be made in this instance? [[Leading->3 - Leading]] [[Opinion->3 - Opinion]] [[Relevance->3 - Relevance]] [[This question is admissible.->Admissible Question Explanation]]Correct! This question is admissable in the court of law. [[Proceed->Speculation Testimony Example]]The former Chief Financial Officer of a major tech company, Arjun Ketkar, is the defendant of a criminal case in which he has been accused of embezzling company funds to purchase a new home after his previous one burned down in a house fire. He is called to the stand by the defense to provide testimony. During direct examination, he insists that he is innocent and that he was not the one responsible for the strange disappearance of company funds. After direct examination ends, an attorney from the prosecution begins to ask Mr. Ketkar questions during cross examination. At one point, he asks, "Losing such an expensive property surely made you more desperate for cash, did it not, Mr. Ketkar?" What objection could be made in this instance? [[Leading->5 - Leading]] [[Badgering->5 - Badgering]] [[Argumentative->Argumentative Explanation]] [[This question is admissible.->5 - Admissible Question]]Correct! The argumentative objection can be made when a question asked by an examiner serves not to extract information from the witness, but rather to advance their own argument. The attorney injects a conclusion into his question: that Mr. Ketkar's financial situation made him desperate for money. Instead of asking Mr. Ketkar how the house fire impacted his financials, the attorney simply asks Mr. Ketkar to agree with his own assessment of the situation, making this an argumentative question. [[Proceed->Nonresponsive Example]](This is a continuation of the previous scenario.) An attorney from the prosecution stands. "Objection, your honor. Nonresponsive." The judge sustains the objection. "Mr. Ketkar," she says, "please answer the counsel for the prosecution's question." Mr. Ketkar grows frustrated. "Can he repeat the question?" The judge nods at the examining attorney. He asks again, "How did the loss of your home impact you financially?" "Badly," Mr. Ketkar says shortly. "As I'm sure you gathered by now. Forgive me, but if you were in my shoes, I'm sure you wouldn't like being probed about your financials much either." What objection could be made in this instance? [[Combative->Combative Explanation]] [[Argumentative->7 - Argumentative]] [[Badgering->7 - Badgering]] [[Nonresponsive->7 - Nonresponsive]]Correct! A witness is considered combative when they are overly aggressive, rude, or confrontational to a questioning attorney, typically during cross examination. In this instance, Mr. Ketkar has already answered the attorney's question ("Badly") when he proceeds to make snide comments about the attorney and his line of questioning. [[Proceed->Rephrase the Question 1]]Congratulations! You've reached the end of this activity. We hope this has helped you gain a better understanding of the many different objections at your disposal --- and how to make the best use of them. Feel free to try the activity again from the beginning to review. You may find it helpful to click each incorrect objection to see explanations as to why they aren't applicable; sometimes, there's considerable grey area that can be hard to glean from intuition alone! If you have any questions regarding this activity, please do not hesistate to reach out to us at specialprograms@hmcsanfran.org. We'll see you at the conference! alt: '', class: 'hmcsf-gavel'}" -- Greetings, attorney! We're so excited to meet you in HMCSF's one and only court simulation --- the District Court committee! In this committee, you will be stepping into the shoes of an attorney and master the art of legal argumentation through life-like mock trials. One of the most valuable skills to develop as a delegate in District Court is making objections. Through effective objections, you can strike down evidence which might jeopardize your argument and strengthen your credibility as a strong attorney. Given how important this skill is, it's vital that you familiarize yourself with objections prior to arriving at HMCSF! That is precisely the purpose of this activity. This activity will walk you through examples of objectionable testimony and help you identify which objections can be made in response. If you haven't already, feel free to consult the Objections Cheat Sheet as you work through this (also available on the District Court page of the HMCSF website). By the end of this exercise, you should be much more comfortable making objections in committee! Without further ado, let's get started! [[Proceed->Leading Example]]Emily Duber, a teacher from a San Francisco high school, witnessed the deadly assault of a student by their peers in a school parking lot. Her direct examiner asks her questions regarding her day leading up to the incident, and she walks the court through her experiences. The examiner then asks, "When you left the building and began to approach your car, you noticed a confrontation happening in the school parking lot, didn't you?" What objection could you make in this instance? [[Speculation->1 - Speculation]] [[Leading->Leading Explanation]] [[Hearsay->1 - Hearsay]] [[Narrative->1 - Narrative]]Correct! A leading question is one in which the examiner strongly hints at the answer they would like to receive from the witness. In most instances, leading questions seek a “yes” or “no” answer (or something to the same effect). Since the attorney's question is a yes-or-no question which, based on the case facts, can only be answered in one way ("yes"), this would be considered a leading question that the attorney must rephrase. [[Proceed->Opinion Example]](This is a continuation of the previous scenario.) An attorney from the defense stands up. "Objection, your honor. Argumentative." The judge sustains the objection, and the attorney changes the question. "How did the loss of your home impact you financially, Mr. Ketkar?" Mr. Ketkar answers. "My apologies, but I fail to see a house fire would lead me to jeopardize my main source of income. Embezzling the money that was going into my payroll anyway would have been idiotic." What objection could be made in this instance? [[Combative->6 - Combative]] [[Speculation->6 - Speculation]] [[Nonresponsive->Nonresponsive Explanation]] [[Opinion->6 - Opinion]]Correct! A witness is considered nonresponsive if they refuse to answer a question or if the answer they provide seeks to dodge or distract from the question being asked. The question Mr. Ketkar was asked was specifically regarding the financial impacts of the house fire; nowehere in Mr. Ketkar's response does he address this point, and he instead volunteers his unsolicited opinion about the prosecution's argument. [[Proceed->Combative Example]]In a murder case, the prosecution calls Gregory Samson, a forensic psychologist, to the stand. During direct examination, the attorney conducting the examination asks Mr. Samson about the defendant's mental state at the moment of the crime. Mr. Samson claims that the defendant seems to have been in a perfectly normal state of mind when he committed the crime. The examination proceeds. In a previous witness's testimony, the witness described certain bullet casings found at the scene in addition to explosive residue indicative of a gunshot. The attorney asks Mr. Samson, "Given the evidence found at the crime scene, which type of gun was used to commit the murder?" What objection could be made in this instance? [[Speculation->2- Speculation]] [[Scope->2 - Scope]] [[Character Evidence->2 - Character Evidence]] [[Opinion->Opinion Explanation]]Correct! The opinion objection can be used when an attorney asks a question which requires expertise or specialized knowledge to answer that the witness does not possess. Even though Mr. Samson is an expert in forensic psychology, he is decidedly not an expert in ballistic analysis. Because of this, the attorney is asking him a question he lacks the expertise to properly answer, making the question susceptible to an opinion objection. [[Proceed->Admissible Question Example]]An attorney for the defense is conducting cross examination on a witness who was present when the defendant performed an armed robbery of a local gas station. "Based on the clothing worn by the defendant during the time of the robbery, you could tell that they were likely poor and in need of money, correct? An attorney for the prosecution stands up. "Objection, your honor. Argumentative." The judge sustains the objection. How should the defense attorney rephrase the question? [["The clothes worn by the defendant didn't look very expensive, did they?"->8 - Incorrect]] [["As they carried out the robbery, what clothes was the defendant wearing?"->8 - Correct]] [["Based on your recollection, how might a bystander have noticed that the defendant was struggling to make ends meet?"->8 - Incorrect]] [["Given their rugged appearance that night, what economic state did the defendant appear to be in?"->8 - Incorrect]] [[Move on to a different question->8 - Move On]]A teacher files a civil suit against his next-door neighbor after a tree on the neighbor's property fell during inclement weather on the teacher's car, totaling it. Unfortunately, the teacher did not have car insurance and is suing the neighbor to cover the damages. The teacher insists that the neighbor knew that the tree was growing old and at risk of falling in the event of high winds; the neighbor denies this fact. The teacher claims that he had overheard an outdoor conversation a few weeks prior between the neighbor and an inspector, who had come onto the property to assess the risk posed by the tree. During direct examination, the teacher's attorney asks him about this interaction. "What did the inspector tell your neighbor?" An attorney for the plaintiff stands up. "Objection, your honor. Hearsay." The judge sustains the objection. How should the teacher's attorney rephrase the question? [["How did the inspector describe the risk posed by the tree?"->9 - Incorrect]] [["What did the inspector tell your neighbor to do about the tree?"->9 - Incorrect]] [["Were there any details mentioned by the inspector which struck you?"->9 - Incorrect]] [["How did the inspector seem to feel about not cutting down the tree?"->9 - Incorrect]] [[Move on to a different question->9 - Correct]]A woman was attacked by a group of masked individuals in her apartment late at night, leaving her in a critical state. Fortunately, she was able to contact emergency services and was brought back to health after several days in the hospital. Eventually, a few of the perpetrators were detained and their identities were revealed; all of them had connections to the woman's ex-husband, who had lost custody of their children after their divorce. Criminal charges were brought against the ex-husband on reasonable suspicion that he had hired the attackers to brutalize his ex-wife. The woman agreed to testify as a witness for the prosecution. At the end of direct examination, an attorney from the prosecution asks the woman, "Can you describe the connection between your ex-husband and the men who attacked you?" The woman answers, "They were all friends of his whom he would invite to the house to play poker. They'd always whisper about things so that I couldn't hear; they were probably talking about something illegal, I'm sure, and it makes sense that after gaining custody of his kids I'd become one of their targets." An attorney for the defense stands up. "Objection, your honor. Speculation." The judge sustains the objection. How can the woman rephrase the last sentence of her testimony? [["In addition to poker, my ex-husband always seemed to be up to something shady with those men; though I can't say exactly what that shady business was, I'm sure it played a big role in the attack on me."->10 - Incorrect]] [["Outside of poker, I don't know much about their connection; all I know is that my ex-husband would never entertain conversation when I'd ask him about them."->10 - Correct]] [["They wouldn't ever let me in on games --- and, for that matter, they'd nnever let me in on their conversations --- and I imagine it was during those games where the conspiracy against my life took shape."->10 - Incorrect]] [["Whenever they were around, he'd get all secretive and quiet, like he was trying to hide something from me."->10 - Incorrect]]Correct! If the witness infers or theorizes on case details without having the appropriate expert background to do so, their testimony can be stricken from the record by a speculation objection. In this instance, Ms. McMillan is perfectly within her right to describe what she observed about the girlfriend's behavior (shaky breathing, disoriented speech); however, her final statement in which she claims that the boyfriend must have "physically harmed" the girlfriend goes beyond what Ms. McMillan directly observed and falls squarely within the realm of speculation. [[Proceed->Argumentative Example]](This is a continuation of the previous scenario.) Ms. McMillan begins to describe the friend's behavior to the court. "She had really shaky breathing, and half of her sentences would trail off without completing a thought. It was hard to understand what she was trying to tell me. But she was frazzled, to be sure. I'm quite sure that [the girlfriend] had just told her about everything that had happened, and hearing that story was probably traumatizing." What objection could be made in this instance? [[Speculation->Speculation Explanation]] [[Opinion->4 - Opinion]] [[Hearsay->4 - Hearsay]] [[Nonresponsive->4 - Nonresponsive]]
